Results.

The results of experiments are given in
Table

1
.
For each benchmark, we list the number of timeouts (i.e., unsolved formulae), the total run time (including timeouts), and also the run time on the successfully decided formulae.
The results show that from all tools,
Noodler
has the lowest number of timeouts
on the aggregation of all benchmarks (42 timeouts in total) and also on each
individual benchmark
(except
Str 2
where it is the second lowest, 3 against 1).
Furthermore, in all benchmarks except
Slog
,
Noodler
is faster than other tools (and for
Slog
it is the second).
The results for
Sloth
on
Str 2
are omitted because
Sloth
was incorrect on
this benchmark (the benchmark is not straight-line) and the results for
Retro
on
Slog
are omitted because
Retro
does not support regular constraints.

Figure 2
:
Times for solving the hardest 1,023 formulae for the tools on
PyEx-Hard

In
Fig.

3
, we provide scatter plots comparing the run times of

Noodler
with the best competitors,
CVC5
and
Z3str4
, on the
PyEx-Hard
benchmark (scatter plots for the other benchmarks are less interesting and can
be found in
Appendix

0.J
).
We can see that there is indeed a large number of benchmarks where
Noodler
is
faster than both competitors (and that the performance of
Noodler
is more
stable, which may be caused by the heuristics in the other tools not
always working well).
Notice that
Noodler
and
CVC5
are on this benchmark complementary: they have
both some timeouts, but each formula is solved by at least one of the tools.

Moreover, in
Fig.

2
, we provide a graph showing times needed to solve 1,023 most difficult formulae for the tools on the
PyEx-Hard
benchmark.

(a)

Noodler
vs.
CVC5
.

(b)

Noodler
vs.
Z3str4
.

Figure 3
:
The performance of
Noodler
and other tools on
PyEx-Hard
.
Times are given in seconds, axes are logarithmic.
Dashed lines represent timeouts (60 s).

Discussion.

The results of the experiments show that our algorithm (even in its prototype
implementation in Python) can beat well established solvers such as
CVC5
,

Z3
, and
Z3str4
.
In particular, it can solve more benchmarks, and also the average time for
(successfully) solving a benchmark is low (as witnessed by the “time
-
T/O”
column in
Table

1
).
The scatter plots also show that it is often complementary to other solvers.

7
Related Work

Our algorithm is an improvement of the automata-based algorithm first proposed in
[
32
]
,
which is, at least in part, used as the basis of several string solvers, namely,

Norn

[
32
,
12
,
10
]
,

Trau

[
22
,
33
,
34
,
35
]
,

OSTRICH

[
9
,
13
,
23
]
,
and
Z3str3RE

[
20
]
.
The original algorithm first transforms equations to the disjunction of their solved forms
[
14
]
through generating alignments of variable boundaries on the equation sides (essentially an incomplete version of Makanin’s algorithm).
Second, it eliminates concatenation from regular constraints
by
automata splitting
.
The algorithm replaces
x\cdot y\in L
by a disjunction of cases

x\in L_{x}\land y\in L_{y}
, one case for each state of
L
’s automaton.
Each disjunct later entails testing emptiness of
L_{x}\cap\mathsf{Lang}(x)
and

L_{y}\cap\mathsf{Lang}(y)
by the automata product construction.

Trau
uses this algorithm within an unsatisfiability check.

Trau
’s main solution finding algorithm also performs a step similar to our
refinement, though with languages underapproximated as arithmetic formulae
(representing their Parikh images).

Sloth

[
22
]
implements a compact version of automata splitting through alternating automata.

OSTRICH
has a way of avoiding the variable boundary alignment for the straight-line formulae, although still uses it outside of it.

Z3str3RE
optimises the algorithm of
[
32
]
heavily by the use of length-aware heuristics.

The two levels of disjunctive branching (transformation into solved form and
automata splitting) are costly.
For instance, for
xyx=zu\land z\in a(ba)^{*}\land u\in(baba)^{*}a

(a subformula of the example in
Section

2
),
there would be 14 alignments/solved forms, e.g. those characterised using
lengths as follows:
(1)
|zu|=0
;
(2)
|y|=|zu|
;
(3)
|x|<|z|,|y|=0
;
(4)
|xy|<z,|y|>0
;
(5)
|x|<|z|,|xy|>z
;
…In the case (5) alone—corresponding to the solved form
z=z_{1}z_{2},u=u_{1}z_{1},x=z_{1},y=z_{2}u_{1}
—automata splitting would generate 15 cases from
z_{1}z_{2}\in\mathsf{Lang}(z)
and
u_{1}u_{2}\in\mathsf{Lang}(u)
, each entailing one intersection emptiness
check (the NFAs for
z
and
u
have 3 and 5 states respectively).
There would be about a hundred of such cases overall.
On the contrary, our algorithm generates only 9 of equivalent cases, 7 if optimised (see
Section

2
).

Our algorithm has an advantage also over pure automata splitting, irrespective of aligning equations. For instance, consider the constraint
xyx\in L\land x\in\mathsf{Lang}(x)\land y\in\mathsf{Lang}(y)
.
Automata splitting generates a disjunction of
n^{2}
constraints
x\in L_{x}\land y\in L_{y}
, with
n
being the number of states of the automaton for
L
,
each constraint with emptiness checks for
\mathsf{Lang}(x)\cap L_{x}
and
\mathsf{Lang}(y)\cap L_{y}
.
Our algorithm avoids generating much of these cases by intersecting with the languages of
\mathsf{Lang}(x)
and
\mathsf{Lang}(y)
early—the construction of
\mathsf{Lang}(x)\cdot\mathsf{Lang}(y)\cdot\mathsf{Lang}(x)
prunes much of
L
’s automaton immediately.
For instance, if
L=(ab)^{*}a^{+}(abcd)^{*}
(its NFA has 7 states) and
\mathsf{Lang}(x)=(a+b)^{*}
, automata splitting explores
7^{2}=49
cases while our algorithm
explores 9 (7 when optimised) of these cases—it would compute the same
product and noodles as in
Section

2
, essentially ignoring the
disjunct
(abcd)^{*}
of
L
.

Approaches and tools for string solving are numerous and diverse,
with various representations of constraints, algorithms, or sorts of inputs.
Many approaches use automata, e.g.,

Stranger

[
31
,
36
,
37
]
,
Norn

[
32
,
12
]
,

OSTRICH

[
9
,
13
,
23
,
38
,
39
]
,

Trau

[
10
,
33
,
34
,
35
]
,

Sloth

[
22
]
,

Slog

[
29
]
,
Slent
[
40
]
,

Z3str3RE

[
20
]
,

Retro

[
25
]
,
ABC
[
41
,
42
]
,
Qzy
[
43
]
, or
BEK
[
44
]
.
Around word equations are centered tools such as CVC4/5
[
45
,
46
,
47
,
48
,
26
,
49
,
50
]
,

Z3

[
51
,
19
]
,
S3
[
52
]
,

Kepler
22

[
28
]
,
StrSolve
[
53
]
,
Woorpje
[
54
]
;
bit vectors are (among other things) used in
Z3Str/2/3/4
[
55
,
56
,
57
,
58
]
,
HAMPI
[
59
]
;
PASS uses arrays
[
60
]
;
G-strings
[
61
]
and GECODE+S
[
62
]
use SAT-solving.
Most of these tools and methods handle much wider range of string constraints than equations and regular constraints.
Our algorithm is not a complete alternative but a promising basis that could improve some of the existing solvers and become a core of a new one.
With regard to equations and regular constraints, the fragment of chain-free constraints
[
10
]
that we handle,
handled also by
Trau
,
is the largest for which any string solvers offers formal completeness guarantees,
with the exception of quadratic equations, handled, e.g., by
[
25
,
28
]
, which are incomparable but of a smaller practical relevance (although some tools actually implement Nielsen’s algorithm
[
11
]
to handle simple quadratic cases).
The other solvers guarantee completeness on smaller fragments, notably that of
OSTRICH
(straight-line),
Norn
, and
Z3str3RE
;
or use incomplete heuristics that work in practice (giving up guarantees of termination, over or under-approximating by various means).
Most string solvers tend to avoid handling regular expressions, by means of postponing them as much as possible or
abstracting them into arithmetic/length and other constraints (e.g.
Trau
,
Z3str3RE
,
Z3str4
, CVC4/5, S3).
A major point of our work is that taking the opposite approach may work even better when automata are approached from the right angle and implemented carefully, though, heuristics that utilise length information or Parikh images would most probably speed up our algorithm as well.
The main selling point of our approach is its efficiency compared to the others, demonstrated on benchmark sets used in other works.

8
Conclusion and Future Work

We have presented a new algorithm for solving a fragment of word equations with
regular constraints, complete in
\mathtt{SAT}
cases and for the chain-free fragment.
It is based on a tight interconnection of equations with regular constraints and built around a novel characterisation of satisfiability of a string constraint through the notion of stability.
We have experimentally shown that the algorithm is very competitive with existing solutions, better especially on difficult examples.

We plan to continue from here towards a complete string solver.
This involves including other types of constraints and coming up with a mature and optimised implementation.
The core algorithm might also be optimised by using a more compact automata representation of noodles that would eliminate redundancies.

Appendix 0.A
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

Assume a single-equation system
\Phi\colon y_{1}\dots y_{n}=z_{1}\dots z_{m}\land\bigwedge_{x\in\mathbb{X}}x\in\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)

where
\mathbb{X}=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{m}\}
. In this section, we sometimes use
(e,\mathsf{Lang})
to denote the single-equation system.
Recall that
L^{\min}
denotes the words that have the minimum length in the
language
L
.

For a word
v=a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}
and two indices
0<i\leq j\leq\ell
, we write
v[i:j]
to denote
the
infix

a_{i}\cdots a_{j}
of
v
between
i
and
j
.
We also write
v[i]
to denote the
i
th character
a_{i}
.

Most of the proof is concerned with proving a simpler version of
Theorem

4.1
,
in which each language
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x),x\in\mathbb{X}
consists of words of the same minimal length
\ell_{x}
. The simplified version of
Theorem

4.1
is formulated as the following lemma.

Lemma 1

If
\Phi
is strongly min-stable, then it has a solution.

The next lemma explains why
Lemma

1
implies
Theorem

4.1
.
Let
L^{\min}
denote the words that have the minimum length in the language
L
.

Lemma 2

If
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(y_{n})=\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(z_{m})
, then

\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}^{\min}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}^{\min}(y_{n})=\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}^{\min}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}^{\min}(z_{m})
.

Proof

(Idea)
From the equality of the languages, every concatenation
u_{1}\cdots u_{n}
of minimum length words on the left must have an equivalent counterpart
v_{1}\cdots v_{m}
on the right, and vice versa.
The words on the right must be minimal too since otherwise one could compose a shorter word on the right, and its counterpart on the left would be shorter then
u_{1}\cdots u_{n}
, which contradicts that
u_{1}\cdots u_{n}
are minimal.
∎

See
4.1

Proof

Corollary of
Lemmas

1
and
2
.
∎

To prove
Lemma

1
, we need to introduce some additional notation and prove some auxiliary Lemmas.

0.A.1
Proof of
Lemma

1

Let us fix a min-length system
\Phi=(e,\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi})
where
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
contains the shortest strings with the length
\ell_{x}
. Unless stated otherwise,
\Phi
will implicitly be the system of concern in this section.
Let
\ell_{\Phi}
be the
length of
\Phi
, the sum

\ell_{\Phi}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{y_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{z_{i}}
. We call a number
p
between

1
and
\ell_{\Phi}
a
position
, and we call the elements of the set
\mathcal{A}=\{(x,i)\mid x\in\mathbb{X},1\leq i\leq\ell_{x}\}

atoms
.
We say that

(y_{k},j)
with
k\leq n,j\leq\ell_{y_{k}}
is the
left atom at the position

p=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\ell_{y_{i}}+j
and write
\mathit{leftAtom}(p)=(y_{k},j)
.
Conversely,
(z_{k},j)
with
k\leq m,j\leq\ell_{z_{k}}
is the
right atom at the position

p=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\ell_{z_{i}}+j
,
written
\mathit{rightAtom}(p)=(z_{k},j)
, and
\mathit{atom}(p)=\{\mathit{leftAtom}(p),\mathit{rightAtom}(p)\}
.
We lift
\mathit{leftAtom}
,
\mathit{rightAtom}
, and
\mathit{atom}
to sets of positions
P
in the standard manner. We define also the set of positions of a set of atoms
A
as
\mathit{pos}(A)=\{p\mid\mathit{atom}(p)\cap A\neq\emptyset\}
. We will write simply
\mathit{pos}(\alpha)
to denote
\mathit{pos}(\{\alpha\})
.

Let us define the relation
{\sim}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}
as the smallest equivalence which relates any two atoms that share a position, i.e., they both belong to
\mathit{atom}(p)
for some position
p
.
An
atom class
is then an equivalence class of
\sim
. We will denote by

[\alpha]_{\sim}
the atom class containing the atom
\alpha
.

We say that atoms in a set
A\subseteq\mathcal{A}

are in agreement
, or that
A

agrees
,
if there is an assignment
\nu
and a symbol
a\in\Sigma
such that for every atom
(x,i)\in A
,
\nu(x)[i]=a
. We may also say that
A

agrees on the letter
a
in the assignment
\nu
.

It is not hard to see that
an assignment is a solution if and only if every atom class agrees in it.
The core of our argument is a proof that any single atom class
[\alpha]_{\sim}
agrees (note that this does not yet imply that all the classes agree in the same assignment, that will need an additional argument).

Lemma 3

If
S
is stable, then any single atom class agrees in it.

To prove
Lemma

3
, will for a given atom class
[\alpha]_{\sim}
construct a sequence
A_{0},\ldots,A_{k}=[\alpha]_{\sim}
of sets of atoms that agree and eventually converge to
[\alpha]_{\sim}
. The proof we will need two components.
First, to ensure that the sequence terminates, we will argue that each
A_{i+1}
is smaller then
A_{i}
in certain well ordering
\succ
on sets of atoms. The ordering is defined as follows.
For each
x\in\mathbb{X}
, we define
seq_{A}(x)
as the sequence of its indices
\{i\mid(x,i)\in A\}
ordered from the smallest on the left.
Let
<_{\mathrm{lex}}
be the usual lexicographic ordering on such sequences (e.g.,
12379<_{\mathrm{lex}}12468
).
Given another set of atoms
B
, we define
A\succeq_{x}B
as
seq_{A}(x)<_{\mathrm{lex}}seq_{B}(x)
,
and we let
A\succ B
if and only if a) for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
,
A\succeq_{x}B
, and b) for at least one
x\in\mathbb{X}
,
B\not\succeq_{x}A
(i.e.,
A\succeq_{x}B
holds for all variables and is sharp for at least one).
It is not difficult to show that
\succ
is a well ordering (It is transitive and antisymmetric. It has a finite domain, hence it has no infinite decreasing sequences.).

The second component needed in the proof of
Lemma

3
is a way of constructing
A_{i+1}
from
A_{i}
.
It is the core of the entire proof and it is summarised in the following lemma.

Lemma 4

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and
A\subset[\alpha]_{\sim}
that agrees, there is
A^{\prime}\subseteq[\alpha]_{\sim}
with
A\succ A^{\prime}
.

Proof

We call a position
p
a
hole
of
A
if
|\mathit{atom}(p)\cap A|=1
, that is, exactly one of the two atoms at
p
is present in
A
.
The
missing atom
of the hole
p
of
A
is the atom
\alpha\in\mathit{atom}(p)\setminus A
(note that a hole has exactly one missing atom).
Since
A
is strictly smaller then
[\alpha]_{\sim}
, it must have a hole (roughly, if there is no hole, then
\sim
restricted to pairs incident with
A
does not contain any pair incident with atoms outside
A
, which means that
A
is an equivalence class of
\sim
, and this contradicts that
A
is strictly smaller than the equivalence class
[\alpha]_{\sim}
).
Let
\boxtimes
be the smallest hole in
A
and let
(x,k)
be its missing atom.
We will present the proof for the case that the missing atom is on the right, i.e.,
(x,k)=\mathit{rightAtom}(\boxtimes)
. The case
(x,k)=\mathit{leftAtom}(\boxtimes)
can be proved analogously.

The new atom class will be constructed in the form

A^{\prime}=A\cup\{(x,k)\}\setminus\{(x,l)\mid k<l\leq\ell_{x}\}.

It is easy to prove that
A\succ A^{\prime}
(for all variables
y\in\mathbb{X}
except
x
,
A\succeq_{y}A^{\prime}
holds since
A^{\prime}
has the same
y
-atoms as
A
, and
A\succ_{x}A^{\prime}
follows from how
A^{\prime}
is constructed from
A
and the def. of
\succeq_{x}
).
In the rest of the proof, we will concentrate on the most difficult part, which is showing that
A^{\prime}
agrees.

Assume that
A
agrees on
a
in an assignment
\nu
.
Let
w=\nu(y_{1})\cdots\nu(y_{n})
be the word obtained using the
\nu
-values on the left side of the equation.
Then, from
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(y_{n})=\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(z_{m})
, there must be words
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
from the right languages

\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(z_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(z_{m})
, respectively, such that
w=v_{1}\cdots v_{m}

(note that this sequence of words does not correspond to an assignment since different occurrences of a variable may have different values).
Let
\boxtimes
appear withing the
j
th word
v_{j}
in the sequence
v_{1}\cdots v_{m}
, that is,

\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\ell_{z_{i}}<\boxtimes\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}\ell_{z_{i}}
.
Note that the position
\boxtimes-k+1
corresponds to the first character of
j
th word in the concatenation,
and that positions within this occurrence of
v_{j}
can be translated to letters of
v_{j}
by

(
*
)

w[\boxtimes-k+i]=v_{j}[i]
for all
i:1\leq i\leq\ell_{x}

and that they also have the corresponding atoms of
x
on the right, i.e.

(
**
)

\mathit{rightAtom}(\boxtimes-k+i)=(x,i)
for all
i:1\leq i\leq\ell_{x}
.

We will show that when
\nu^{\prime}
is constructed from
\nu
as
\nu^{\prime}=\nu\setminus\{x\mapsto\nu(x)\}\cup\{x\mapsto v_{j}\}
, then
A^{\prime}
agrees on
a
in
\nu^{\prime}
.
For that, we will first argue that
v_{j}[i]=a
for all
i
s.t.
(x,i)\in A
and
1\leq i<k
, and then we will show that
v_{j}[k]=a
as well.

To show that
v_{j}[i]=a
for all
i
s.t.
(x,i)\in A,1\leq i<k
, we show that

w[p]=a
for all positions in
P=\{\boxtimes-k+i\mid 1\leq i<k,(x,i)\in A\}
(the implication follows from
(*)
).
Since
A
agrees on
a
in
\nu
,
the words
\nu(y_{1}),\ldots,\nu(y_{n})
have
a
on all indices corresponding to the atoms in
A
, that is,
\nu(y_{j})[i]=a
for all
1\leq j\leq n
and
i
s.t.
(y_{j},i)\in A
.
Therefore, for all positions
p
, we have
\mathit{leftAtom}(p)\in A\implies w[p]=a
.
The definition of
\boxtimes
says that for all positions
p
smaller than
\boxtimes
,

\mathit{leftAtom}(p)\in A\Longleftrightarrow\mathit{rightAtom}(p)\in A
.
This equivalence applies to all positions of
P
because they are smaller then
\boxtimes
by definition.
In summary, we have that for all
p\in P
,
we have
p=\boxtimes-k+i
for some
i:1\leq i<k\land(x,i)\in A
, that

(x,i)=\mathit{rightAtom}(p)
by
(**)
, and that
\mathit{rightAtom}(p)\in A\implies\mathit{leftAtom}(p)\in A\implies w[p]=a
, which gives
w[p]=a
for all
p\in P
.

We show that
v_{j}[k]=a
by a similar argument.
We know that
\boxtimes
has an atom from
A
on the left (by definition of a hole and our initial assumption that the right atom is missing),
hence
w[\boxtimes]=a
, and because
w[\boxtimes]=v_{j}[k]
by (
*
), we have
v_{j}[k]=a
.

We have shown that
\nu^{\prime}=\nu\setminus\{x\mapsto\nu(x)\}\cup\{x\mapsto v_{j}\}
can be used as an assignment in which
A^{\prime}
agrees on
a
.

∎

With
Lemma

4
at hand, we can prove
Lemma

3
.

Proof
(
Lemma

3
)

Given arbitrary
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
,
we will inductively construct the sequence
A_{0},\ldots,A_{k}=[\alpha]_{\sim}
of sets of atoms, that will for each
0\leq i<0
satisfy two conditions:

1.

A_{i}\subseteq[\alpha]_{\sim}
,

2.

A_{i}\succ A_{i+1}
.

For
i=0
,
A_{0}
can be chosen as any singleton
\{\beta\}\subseteq[\alpha]_{\sim}
.
It satisfies both conditions:

A_{0}\subseteq[\alpha]_{\sim}
is trivial, and since
A_{0}
is a
singleton, it trivially agrees in any assignment.
The induction step then follows in case
A_{i}
is not yet equal to
[\alpha]_{\sim}
.
In that case, we can use
Lemma

4
to derive that there is the needed
A_{i+1}
.
The construction terminates since
\succ
is a well ordering. The only way how it can terminate is by
A_{k}=[\alpha]_{\sim}
(this is the only possible situation in which
Lemma

4
does induce existence of the next set in the sequence).
∎

With
Lemma

3
at hand, we can now finish the proof of
Lemma

1
.

Proof
(
Lemma

1
)

Consider all equivalence classes
\mathcal{E}=\{[\alpha_{1}]_{\sim},\ldots,[\alpha_{k}]_{\sim}\}
of

\sim
. We will construct a sequence of language assignments
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}=\mathsf{Lang}^{0},\ldots,\mathsf{Lang}^{k}
,and a sequence of letters
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
such that for each
i:0\leq i\leq k
,
the following holds:

1.

\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)
is a nonempty subset of
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
,

2.

(e,\mathsf{Lang}^{i})
is a system, that is,
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(y_{n})=\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(z_{m})
,

3.

in the system
(e,\mathsf{Lang}^{i})
, all classes
[\alpha_{j}]_{\sim},1\leq j\leq i
agree on
a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}
, respectively, in every assignment (notice that this condition is vacuous for
i=0
).

For each
i:0\leq i<k
, we construct
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}
from
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}
so that
for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
,
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(x)
contains the words of
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)
due to which
[\alpha_{i}]_{\sim}
could agree on
a_{i}
—those with
a_{i}
on positions marked by the atoms in
[\alpha_{i}]_{\sim}
, formally,

\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(x)=\{w\in\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)\mid\forall j:(x,j)\in[\alpha_{i+1}]_{\sim}\rightarrow w[j]=a_{i+1}\}.

Let us now argue by induction on
i
that the points 1 to 3 above hold.
For
i=0
,
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}=\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}
. Points 1 and 2 are obviously satisfied, 3 as well since it is is vacuous for
i=0
.

Assume that 1-3 hold for
i
and let us prove that they hold for
i+1
.
We first prove that 1 holds.
First,
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
follows from that 1 holds for
i
, that is,
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
, and from that
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)
by construction.
Second,
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(x)\neq\emptyset
follows from that

\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)\neq\emptyset
by IH and that
[\alpha_{i}]_{\sim}
agrees on
a_{i}
in
(e,\mathsf{Lang}^{i})
(the definition of agreement implies that
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)
must have at least one word with
w[j]=a_{i}
for all
(x,j)\in[\alpha_{i}]_{\sim}
, and that word will appear in
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(x)
).

Next, we prove point 2.
We will show the proof only for
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(y_{n})\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(z_{m})
. The opposite inclusion may be proved analogously. Consider
some
w\in\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(y_{n})
. From IH we have
w\in\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(y_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(y_{n})=\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(z_{m})
.
We can write
w
as
w=w_{y_{1}}\cdots w_{y_{n}}=w_{z_{1}}\cdots w_{z_{m}}
where
w_{x}\in\mathsf{Lang}^{i}(x)
for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
.
Assume for the sake of contradiction that
w\not\in\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(z_{1})\cdots\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(z_{m})
.
That means that for some
j
,
w_{z_{j}}\not\in\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(z_{j})
.
By definition of
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(z_{j})
, there must be an atom
(z_{j},l)\in[\alpha_{i+1}]_{\sim}
such that
w_{z_{j}}[l]\neq a_{i+1}
.
The other, left, atom (on the position
p=\sum_{o=1}^{o\leq j}\ell_{o}+l
) also belongs in the equivalence class
[\alpha_{i+1}]_{\sim}
by definition of
\sim
. Let it be some
(y_{p},q)
. We have that
w_{y_{p}}[q]
is not
a_{i+1}
, but this contradicts the definition of
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}(y_{p})
.

Last, we prove point 3. In the system
(e,\mathsf{Lang}^{i})
,
[\alpha_{i+1}]_{\sim}
agrees on some
a_{i+1}
by
Lemma

3
.
By the IH, all
[\alpha_{j}]_{\sim},1\leq j<i
agree on
a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}
, respectively, in all assignments.
\mathsf{Lang}^{i+1}
is then constructed by removing words from
\mathsf{Lang}^{i}
that do not have the
a_{i+1}
on the positions of the atoms in
[\alpha_{i+1}]_{\sim}
.
Therefore, after the removal, only assignments in which
[\alpha_{i+1}]_{\sim}
agrees on
a_{i+1}
are are left.
We have thus proved that the properties 1-3 are satisfied for each
i:0\leq i\leq k
.

The solution of the original system
(e,\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi})
is now obtained as follows.
In
\mathsf{Lang}^{k}
, every language
\mathsf{Lang}^{k}(x),x\in\mathbb{X}
equals the singleton
\{w_{x}\}
such that for each
i:1\leq i\leq\ell_{x}
,

w_{x}[j]=a_{i}
where
(x,i)\in[\alpha_{i}]_{\sim}
(since 3 holds for
L^{k}
). We also know that
w_{x}\in\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
(since 2 holds for all
0\leq i\leq k
). The map
\nu=\{x\mapsto w_{x}\mid x\in\mathbb{X}\}
is then an assignment in
(e,\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi})
, and it is also a solution because all atom classes agree in it (since 3 holds).
∎

Appendix 0.B
Proof of
Theorem

4.3

We start by defining some notation.
Given an inclusion graph
G
, a
strongly connected component
(SCC)
of
G
is a maximal subgraph of
G
where each two vertices are
reachable.
An SCC is
trivial
if it has exactly one vertex (which does not contain
a self-loop) and
non-trivial
otherwise.
An SCC
C
is
terminal
if the set of vertices reachable from
C

equals
C
.
Given an SCC
C
of
G
, then
\mathit{Vars}(C)
is the set of all variables that
occur in the vertices of
C
.

We first start with proving the following lemma that gives us some properties of
inclusion graphs.

Lemma 5

Let
G=(V,E)
be an inclusion graph of
\Phi
.
Then the following holds:

1.

Every non-trivial SCC of
G
is terminal.

2.

Let
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
be two different non-trivial SCCs
of
G
, then
\mathit{Vars}(C_{1})\cap\mathit{Vars}(C_{2})=\emptyset
.

3.

If
x
is a variable on the right-hand side of some vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
that is a trivial SCC, then
x
does not occur on the
right-hand side of any other vertex of
G
.

Let us proceed to the proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

See
4.3

Proof

(
\Leftarrow
): Trivial by construction of singleton language assignments from
the solution.

(
\Rightarrow
): We will show how to construct a solution of
\Phi
given
a live language assignment
\mathsf{Lang}
that refines
\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}
and is stable
for
G
.
Intuitively, the construction proceeds by computing
partial
string
assignments bottom-up on the structure of
G
, starting from non-trivial
SCCs (using
Theorem

4.1
) and then proceeding upward on the (SCC-free)
structure of
G
, in each step taking the concatenation of the terminal
vertices, removing those vertices, and using
Theorem

4.2
to infer
assignments to other variables.

Formally, let us consider a non-trivial SCC
C
of
G
(we note that due
to
Lemma

5
(
1
),
C
is
terminal).
From Condition (IG
4
), it follows that for every vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
of
C
, there is also the vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s}

in
C
.
Therefore, since
\mathsf{Lang}
is live and stable for
G
(and so also
for
C
), from
Theorem

4.1
it follows that there exists a string
assignment
\nu_{C}
that assigns strings to the variables in
\mathit{Vars}(C)
that
is a solution of
\Phi_{|C}
(where
\Phi_{|C}
is a formula obtained
from
\Phi
by removing all subformulae with variables not occurring in the
vertices of
C
).
We can now build the string assignment
\nu_{0}
by uniting the assignments

\nu_{C}
for all non-trivial SCCs
C
of
G
(this is possible since from

Lemma

5
(
2
), the sets of
variables of two different non-trivial SCCs in
G
are disjoint).
Let us also create the graph
G_{0}
by removing all non-trivial SCCs
from
G
(they are not needed any more since we have their solutions
in
\nu_{0}
).
We note that
G_{0}
is acyclic.

We now proceed by constructing a sequence of partial solutions
\nu_{0}\subseteq\nu_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\nu_{n}
, where
\nu_{n}
is
a solution of
\Phi
, as follows. We start by assigning
i\leftarrow 0
and
keep repeating the following steps until
G_{i}
is empty.

1.

First, we create the language assignment
\mathsf{Lang}_{i}
such that

\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}\{w\}&\text{if }(x\mapsto w)\in\nu_{i},\\
\mathsf{Lang}(x)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(5)

2.

Now, we pick from
G_{i}
the vertices to process in this iteration.
Let
T_{i}=\{\!\hbox to37.79pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.89468pt\lower-3.1298pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.18752pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!,\ldots,\!\hbox to37.63pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.81693pt\lower-3.1298pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.10976pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\}
be the largest set of terminal vertices
of
G_{i}
such that for all
1\leq j\leq k
, all variables
in
s_{j}
either

(i)

are assigned a string in
\nu_{i}
or

(ii)

do not occur on the
right-hand side of any vertex in
G_{i}
.

We claim that
T_{i}
is unique and non-empty (we give the claim formally
below as
Claim
).

3.

Using
T_{i}
, we construct the single-equation system
\varphi_{i}\colon s_{1}\sharp\ldots\sharp s_{k}=t_{1}\sharp\ldots\sharp t_{k}\land\bigwedge_{x\in\mathbb{X}}x\in\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
.
We claim that
\mathsf{Lang}_{i}
is weakly stable for
\varphi_{i}
(we formally
give the claim and its proof below as
Claim
).
Using the claim, we can use
Theorem

4.2
(especially
the algorithm in its proof) to obtain a solution
\nu^{\prime}
to
\varphi_{i}
.
We then set
\nu_{i+1}\leftarrow\nu_{i}\cup\nu^{\prime}
.

4.

Next, we set
G_{i+1}
to be the same as
G_{i}
without the
vertices (and the incident edges) from
T_{i}
.

5.

Finally,
i\leftarrow i+1
.

Let us use
n
for the last value of
i
.
Before we continue, we give proofs of the two claim used above.
First, we show that each
T_{i}
is unique and non-empty (which is also the
reason why the algorithm above always terminates).

Claim

For all
0\leq i\leq n
it holds that
T_{i}
is unique and non-empty.

Proof:

•

(
non-emptiness
)
First, we show that
T_{i}
is non-empty by contradiction: assume that
G_{i}

is non-empty and that for every
G_{i}
-node
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
,
there is an occurrence of a variable
x
in
s
such that

(i)

x
is not assigned a string by
\nu_{i}
and

(ii)

x
occurs on the right-hand side of some vertex

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t^{\prime}}

(and so
T_{i}
is empty).
Then, according to Condition (IG
3
) of inclusion graphs, it follows
that there is an edge from
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
to

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t^{\prime}}
.
Because the previous condition holds for all nodes in
G_{i}
, it follows
that there is a cycle in
G_{i}
, which is a contradiction with the fact
that
G_{i}
is acyclic.

•

(
uniqueness
)
For the sake of contradiction, assume that there are two different
non-empty sets
T_{i}^{1}
and
T_{i}^{2}
that are largest and satisfy the
requirements.
But then their union
T_{i}^{1}\cup T_{i}^{2}
would also satisfy the
requirements, which is a contradiction with
T_{i}^{1}
and
T_{i}^{2}
being
largest.

\blacksquare

Next, we prove the claim that allowed application of
Theorem

4.2

above.

Claim

For all
0\leq i\leq n
it holds that

\mathsf{Lang}_{i}
is weakly stable for
\varphi_{i}
.

Proof:
In order to show that
\mathsf{Lang}_{i}
is weakly stable for
\varphi_{i}
, we
need to show the following two properties:

(i)

\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(s_{1}\sharp\ldots\sharp s_{k})\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(t_{1}\sharp\ldots\sharp t_{k})
:
We prove the property by showing that for each vertex

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
in
G
, we have

\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(t)
.
We proceed by induction on
i
.

(a)

i=0
:
Let
Y
be the set of variables occurring in non-trivial SCCs
of
G
.
Then, from the construction,
\mathsf{Lang}_{0}
assigns each variable
y\in Y
a value obtained from the solution of non-trivial
SCCs
\nu_{0}
.
As a consequence, for every vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
of
a non-trivial SCC in
G
, it holds that
\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(s)=\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(t)
, which is even stronger than
\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(t)
.

Furthermore, for every vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s^{\prime}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t^{\prime}}

that is in the acyclic part of
G
and that contains some
variables from
Y
on its left-hand side
2
2

2

Note that due to

Lemma

5
(
3
), it
cannot happen that any variable from
Y
would be on the
right-hand side of a node in the acyclic part of
G
.
,
we know that for any variable
y\in Y
, it holds that

\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(y)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}(y)
(
\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(y)
is, in fact,
a singleton) and, therefore, since it originally
held (from the assumption) that
\mathsf{Lang}(s^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}(t^{\prime})
, it will also hold that
\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(s^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}(t^{\prime})
(note that due to

Lemma

5
(
3
), it
follows that
\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(t^{\prime})=\mathsf{Lang}(t^{\prime})
since
t^{\prime}

contains no variable from
Y
, so we can conclude that

\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(s^{\prime})=\mathsf{Lang}_{0}(t^{\prime})
).

(b)

i>0
:
Our induction hypothesis is that for every vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
in
G
, it holds that
\mathsf{Lang}_{i-1}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{i-1}(t)
.
Let us now look at the inclusions represented by vertices
from
T_{i}
.
By construction,
\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(x)\subsetneq\mathsf{Lang}_{i-1}(x)
can only happen
for variables
x
that do not appear on the right-hand side of any
vertex in
G_{i}
(in the construction of
T_{i}
, we could not have
picked a vertex containing
x
).
Therefore, we can conclude that
\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(t)
.

(ii)

Each variable
x\in\mathbb{X}
has no more than one
occurrence in
t_{1}\sharp\ldots\sharp t_{k}
.
We prove this by contradiction: assume that variable
x
has more
than one occurrence on the right-hand sides of
T_{i}
, and let one of
the occurrences in the vertex

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{j}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{j}}
.
Then, by Condition (IG
2
),
G
also contains the
vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{j}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{j}}
and, by
Condition (IG
3
), the two vertices are in a non-trivial SCC,
which is a contradiction with the fact that
G_{i}
is acyclic
(which follows from the fact that
G_{0}
is acyclic and that we
only removed vertices/edges when we were creating
G_{i}
in
Step
4
) of the previous iteration of the loop.

\blacksquare

Finally, we need to show that
\nu_{n}
is a solution of
\Phi
.
We leverage the following property from the proof of

Claim
(i): for every
0\leq i\leq n
and every vertex

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
in
G
, it holds that
\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{Lang}_{i}(t)
.
Together with the fact that
\mathsf{Lang}_{n}
assigns each variable a singleton
language, we infer that
\mathsf{Lang}_{n}(s)=\mathsf{Lang}_{n}(t)
.
It follows that
\nu_{n}
is a solution of
\Phi
.
∎

the opposite vertex

\!\hbox to29.79pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.89468pt\lower-3.1298pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\notin G_{\mathcal{E}}

v_{i}\in V^{\prime}
. According to
Claim
we have
that
v_{i}\in V^{\prime}
for each
1\leq i\leq k
. Therefore,
v_{i}\neq v_{j}
for each
i\neq j
meaning that
the cycle
\pi
is in
\mathit{SG_{\mathcal{E}}}
. And hence all vertices from
\pi
belongs to the same (nontrivial) SCC, which
is a contradiction to
v_{i}
belonging to
V^{\prime}
.

Now, we prove the second part of the theorem, i.e., if there is an acyclic inclusion graph for
{\mathcal{E}}
, then
\mathtt{incl}({\mathcal{E}})
is acyclic.
From the definition of chain-freeness we have that
{\mathcal{E}}
is chain-free iff
\mathit{SG_{\mathcal{E}}}
is acyclic. From
Theorem

4.5

we further have that there is an acyclic inclusion graph iff
\mathit{SG_{\mathcal{E}}}
is acyclic. Then, according to
Algorithm

1
we get that for each
v

it does not contain
\mathsf{dual}(v)
. Since
\mathtt{incl}({\mathcal{E}})
is an inclusion graph (proven above), it is acyclic (otherwise it must contain at least
one vertex together with its dual).

The graph
\mathtt{incl}({\mathcal{E}})
has a minimum number of vertices because vertices in nontrivial SCCs in
\mathit{SG_{\mathcal{E}}}
(and between nontrivial SCCs)
forces the inclusion graph to have all also the dual ones.

Appendix 0.D
Proof of
Theorem

4.5

See
4.5

Proof

(
\Rightarrow
)
Given the splitting graph
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}=(P_{\Phi},E_{\Phi},\mathtt{var})
of a system
\Phi\colon\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}=t_{i}\land\bigwedge_{x\in\mathbb{X}}x\in\mathsf{Lang}_{\Phi}(x)
, we show how to construct an acyclic inclusion graph
G

for
\Phi
.
First, we use
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
to construct the

übergraph

UG_{\Phi}
of
\Phi
, which can be seen as a graph
obtained by merging all nodes of
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
corresponding to all
positions on one side of an equation into one (i.e., for a system
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}=t_{i}
, there will be one node for each
s_{i}
and
one node for each
t_{i}
).
Then, we will use the
UG
to choose, for each equation
s_{i}=t_{i}
, whether to add into
G
either

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{i}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{i}}
or
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{i}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{i}}
(adding
both is not allowed since that would imply that there is a cycle
in
G
).

Formally, the übergraph of
\Phi
is a graph
UG_{\Phi}=(\{1,\ldots,2m\},H)
with nodes
\{1,\ldots,2m\}
and edges
H=\{(j_{1},j_{2})\mid((j_{1},k_{1}),(j_{2},k_{2}))\in E_{\Phi}\text{ for some }k_{1},k_{2}\}
.

Claim

\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
is chain-free iff
UG_{\Phi}
is acyclic.

Proof:
(
\Rightarrow
)
By contradiction.
Assume that
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
is chain-free and
UG_{\Phi}
contains
a cycle
j_{0}\to j_{1}\to\ldots\to j_{\ell}\to j_{0}
.
From the definition of edges in a splitting graph, it follows that there is an
edge from
(j_{i},k_{i})
to
(j_{i^{\prime}},k_{i^{\prime}})
in
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
for
i^{\prime}=i+1\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}\ell
iff there is an edge from every
(j_{i},k_{\alpha})
for
1\leq k_{\alpha}\leq n_{j_{i}}
to
(j_{i^{\prime}},k_{i^{\prime}})
.
We can therefore construct a chain
(j_{0},k_{0}),(j_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(j_{\ell},k_{\ell}),(j_{0},k_{0})
in
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
where each
k_{i+1\mathbin{\mathrm{mod}}\ell}
is
a position with an incoming edge from
(j_{i},k_{i})
, which is a contradiction.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Because
UG_{\Phi}
can be seen as an existential abstraction
of
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
, a chain in
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
implies a cycle
in
UG_{\Phi}
.

\blacksquare

Claim

Let
s_{i}=t_{i}
be an equation of
\Phi
and
(2i-1)
and
(2i)
be
the nodes of
UG_{\Phi}
that correspond to
s_{i}
and
t_{i}

respectively.
Then the node
(2i-1)
is a source (resp. a sink) iff the node
(2i)
is a sink
(resp. a source).

Proof:
Let us show that if node
(2i-1)
is a source in
UG_{\Phi}
, then
node
(2i)
is a sink (the other part can be proved similarly).
We proceed by contradiction: assume that
(2i-1)
is a source but
(2i)
is
not a sink, i.e., w.l.o.g., there is an edge
(2i)\to(2h)
.
From the definition of übergraph, it needs to hold that there is an edge

((2i,k_{\alpha}),(2h,k_{\beta}))
in
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
for some
1\leq k_{\alpha}\leq n_{2i}
and
1\leq k_{\beta}\leq n_{2h}
.
Let
s_{i}=t_{i}
and
s_{h}=t_{h}
be the two equations
whose sides correspond to nodes
(2i-1)
,
(2i)
,
(2h-1)
, and
(2h)
respectively.
W.l.o.g, let
\mathtt{var}((2i,k_{\alpha}))=x
and
\mathtt{var}((2h,k_{\beta}))=y
.
For the edge
((2i,k_{\alpha}),(2h,k_{\beta}))
to be in
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
,
it needs to hold that
s_{i}
contains an occurrence of variable
y
, say,
at position
(2i-1,k_{\gamma})
.
Since
y
has an occurrence in
t_{h}
and also in
s_{i}
, there will
also be an edge from
(2h-1)
to
(2i-1)
, which is a contradiction with the
fact that
(2i-1)
is a source.

\blacksquare

Our procedure for constructing the inclusion graph
G
for
\Phi
now
works as follows:

1.

First, let
\mathit{Sinks}
be the set of
sink
nodes
of
UG_{\Phi}
and
\mathit{Sources}
be the set of its
source
nodes.
We also start with an empty inclusion graph
G=(\emptyset,\emptyset)
.

2.

Then we proceed in a loop until
UG_{\Phi}
is empty:

(a)

We pick a pair of nodes
(2i-1)
and
(2i)
such that
(2i-1)
is
a sink and
(2i)
is a source (if it is the other way around, we just
switch the sides of the
i
-th equation).

Claim
and acyclicity of

UG_{\Phi}
(
Claim
) guarantee
that this is possible.
We remove
(2i-1)
and
(2i)
from
UG_{\Phi}
.

(b)

We insert vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{i}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{i}}
into
G

(the two sides of the inclusion correspond to nodes
(2i-1)
and
(2i)

of the übergraph).

(c)

For every node of the modified
UG_{\Phi}
that is not
in
\mathit{Sinks}\cup\mathit{Sources}
, we check if it is a sink (resp. a source) and add it to
\mathit{Sinks}
(resp.
\mathit{Sources}
).

3.

Finally, we set the set of edges of
G
to satisfy
Condition (IG
3
) (this is deterministic).

Claim

G
is an acyclic inclusion graph.

Proof:
Let us show that the conditions of acyclic inclusion graphs hold for
G
.

(IG
1
):

This obviously holds because the construction of
G

from
UG_{\Phi}
proceeds until
UG_{\Phi}
is empty, in
each iteration removing one equation
s_{i}=t_{i}

from
UG_{\Phi}
and adding into
G
either

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{i}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{i}}
or
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{i}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{i}}
.

(acyclicity):

By contradiction: assume that
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}
is chain-free but

.
From the construction of edges in
G
, it needs to hold that
s_{1}

and
t_{2}
share a variable (say
x_{1}
),
s_{2}
and
t_{3}

share a variable (say
x_{2}
), …, and
s_{\ell}
and
t_{1}

share a variable (say
x_{\ell}
).
Looking at the übergraph
UG_{\Phi}
, there will then be
an edge from
t_{1}
to
t_{2}
(since
s_{1}
and
t_{2}

both contain
x_{1}
),
t_{2}
to
t_{3}
, …,

t_{\ell}
to
t_{1}
, creating a cycle in
UG_{\Phi}
.
From
Claim
it follows that
\mathit{SG}_{\Phi}

is not chain-free, which is a contradiction.

(IG
2
):

We show the antecedent of the condition never holds, i.e., that there is
no vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
in
G
such that
t

contains a variable with multiple occurrences on right-hand sides of
vertices of
G
.
We proceed by contradiction and assume that there are multiple occurrences
of some variable
x
on the right-hand sides of vertices in
G
.
Let us analyze the following two possible cases:

(a)

G
contains a vertex
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
with (at
least) two occurrences of
x
in
t
.

Then, in
UG_{\Phi}
, there will be an edge from
s

to
t
.
Therefore, in the algorithm, at some point,
s
will become
a source and
t
will become a sink.
But then the node inserted into
G
will be
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s}
, which is a contradiction with the fact that
G

contains
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
and (acyclicity).

(b)

G
contains two different vertices
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{1}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{1}}

and
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{2}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{2}}
with
x
in both
t_{1}

and
t_{2}
.

The two vertices correspond to two equations
s_{1}=t_{1}
and

s_{2}=t_{2}
.
Since
x
is in
t_{1}
and in
t_{2}
, there will be edges

s_{1}\to t_{2}
and
s_{2}\to t_{1}

in
UG_{\Phi}
.
W.l.o.g., assume that the nodes
s_{1}
and
t_{1}
were removed
from
UG_{\Phi}
before
s_{2}
and
t_{2}
,
adding
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{1}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{1}}
into
G
.
Since
s_{1}
has an outgoing edge and
t_{1}
has an incoming
edge, at best,
s_{1}
can be a source and
t_{1}
can be
a sink, which would mean that we would
add
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{1}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{1}}
to
G
, which is a
contradiction with the fact that
G

contains
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s_{1}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t_{1}}
and (acyclicity).

(IG
3
):

Trivial from the construction.

(IG
4
):

Follows from (acyclicity).

\blacksquare

(
\Leftarrow
)
By contradiction:
let
G
be an acyclic inclusion graph of
\Phi
and assume that there is
a cycle

in
UG_{\Phi}
(by
Claim
, considering
a cycle in the übergraph is sufficient).
Further, assume that the equations whose sides participate in the cycle are

s_{1}=t_{1}
, …,
s_{\ell}=t_{\ell}
.
From the definition of the splitting graph, we know that
t_{1}

and
s_{2}
share a variable (say
x_{1}
),

t_{2}
and
s_{3}
share a variable (say
x_{2}
),
…,
and
t_{\ell}
and
s_{1}
share a variable (say
x_{\ell}
).
From the definition of an inclusion graph, if it is acyclic, then from each
equation
s=t
, there can only be one inclusion in
G

(either
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{s}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{t}
or
\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s}
).
There are then two general ways how the inclusion graph for
\Phi
can look
like:

1.

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{1}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{1}}
,

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{2}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{2}}
,

\vphantom{A_{1}^{2}}\mathsf{t_{\ell}}\!\othersubseteq\!\mathsf{s_{\ell}}
.
But then, from Condition (IG
3
), since

and
s_{2}

share
x_{1}
,
t_{2}
and
s_{3}
share
x_{2}
, …,

and
s_{1}
share
x_{\ell}
, there will be a cycle

(similar reasoning holds for nodes

Or it contains the nodes

), it follows that there is a cycle in
G
, which
is a contradiction with the fact that
G
is acyclic.
∎

Appendix 0.E
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

See
5.1

Proof

We inductively prove the following invariant of the algorithm that need to hold in every iteration of the main
loop: for each
(\mathsf{Aut},W)\in\mathit{Branches}
and
v=\!\hbox to29.79pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.89468pt\lower-3.1298pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.18752pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\in V\setminus W
it holds that
L(\mathsf{Aut}(s))\subseteq L(\mathsf{Aut}(t))
.

1.

Base case: At the beginning
W=V
and therefore the invariant holds trivially.

2.

Inductive case: Assume that the invariant holds for all
(\mathsf{Aut},W)
in
\mathit{Branches}
. We prove that it
is still valid after an iteration of the main loop. Consider
(\mathsf{Aut},W)
on line 3. If the condition on line

3
holds then for
(\mathsf{Aut},W\setminus\{v\})
the invariant clearly holds. We proceed with
the case the condition is not fulfilled. Now assume the case that
s
and
t
do not share a variable. Then,
from the property of
\mathtt{refine}
, we have that for each
\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
:
L(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}(s))\subseteq L(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}(t))
, therefore,
v
need not be included in
W^{\prime}
. From the condition (IG
3
) of the
inclusion graph, we have that only successors of
v
in
G_{\mathcal{E}}
might be affected by the refinement of
s

(other variables remain the same). Hence, for each
(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime},W^{\prime})
where
\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
the invariant holds.
For the case that
s
and
t
share a variable, the reasoning is the same as in the previous case except that
the inclusion
L(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}(s))\subseteq L(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}(t))
might not be true in general. However,
again from (IG
3
) we get that
v
has a self-loop in
G_{\mathcal{E}}
and hence it is included to

W^{\prime}
as well.

Based on the invariant, if the algorithm returns
\mathtt{SAT}
, we have that the
G_{\mathcal{E}}
is stable wrt.
\mathsf{Aut}
(line 4).
From
Theorem

4.3
we then obtain that
\Phi
is satisfiable. Further, we know
that
\mathtt{refine}
preserves solutions. Therefore, for each solution
\nu
of
\Phi
, there is some
(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime})\in\mathit{Branches}

s.t.
\nu(x)\in L(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime\prime}(x))
for each variable
x
. Hence, if the algorithm returns
\mathtt{UNSAT}
,
\mathit{Branches}=\emptyset
, which
means that
\Phi
is unsatisfiable. ∎

Appendix 0.F
Proof of
Theorem

5.2

See
5.2

Proof

In the following, successors of a
(\mathsf{Aut},W)
we mean all
(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime},W^{\prime})
that were transitively obtained
from the refinement of
(\mathsf{Aut},W)
. Therefore, the computation of the algorithm can be seen as an infinite
tree whose vertices are labelled by items from
\mathit{Branches}
.
Since the inclusion graph
G_{\mathcal{E}}
is acyclic, it is possible
do a topological sort of vetices as
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}=V
. From the FIFO used for storing the vertices,
we hence have if
W\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}=\emptyset

for some
(\mathsf{Aut},W)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then for all future successors
(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime},W^{\prime})
of
(\mathsf{Aut},W)

we have
W^{\prime}\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}=\emptyset
. Moreover, since we use FIFO structure in the algorithm and the inclusion graph is acyclic,
each branch leading from
(\mathsf{Aut}_{\Phi},V)
contains either a vertex having no successors (returning

\mathtt{SAT}
or
\mathcal{T}=\emptyset
) or a vertex
(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime},W^{\prime})
where
v_{1}\notin W^{\prime}
. The same reasoning can be
repeated for such vertices for
v_{2}
etc. Therefore, we have if
W\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}=\emptyset
for
some vertex
(\mathsf{Aut},W)
, then all branches leading from this vertex contains either a vertex having no
successors or a vertex
(\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime},W^{\prime})
where
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k},v_{k+1}\}\cap W^{\prime}=\emptyset
where
k<|V|
.
Using this argument, we immediately get that all branches ends with a vertex having no successors, or with a
vertex having
W=\emptyset
and therefore, the algorithm always terminates. The rest is a direct consequence
of
Theorem

5.1
.
∎

Appendix 0.G
Proof of
Theorem

5.3

See
5.3

Proof

(Idea)
Assume that
\Phi
is satisfiable and therefore it has a fixed
solution
\nu
. Then, in each iteration of the algorithm,
we have that the length of a current shortest word of
\mathsf{Aut}(x)
is strictly bigger
than the length of a shortest word of
\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}(x)
(on line 13) or it holds

\mathsf{Aut}^{\prime}(x)^{\min}\subset\mathsf{Aut}(x)^{\min}
. Therefore, after a finite number of
steps we reach,in the worst case, an automata assignment having
\mathsf{Aut}
s.t.

\mathsf{Aut}(x)^{\min}=\{\nu(x)\}
for each variable
x
, which is stable on
line 6 and hence, the algorithm eventually returns
\mathtt{SAT}
.
∎
